 ceṉṉi - 600 006,  maṟṟum pirācacu intiyā, 
 tiruvallikkēṇi - 600 005.
yāṉ aimpatu āṇṭukaḷukku mēlāka ērāḷamāṉa nūlkaḷil ārāyntu kaṇṭa muṭivukaḷi nūlkaḷāka eḻutiyirukkiṉṟēṉ.
pāṟkaṭalik kaṭintu amutam eṭuttatu pōla, uṇmi kāṇum uṇarcciyiṉāl naṭunili niṉṟu ārāyntirukkiṉṟēṉ. 
oruvar tam vāḻnāḷ muḻuvatum kaṟṟālum aṟiyamuṭiyāta uṇmikaḷi ellām eḷitāka eḻuti vittirukkiṉṟēṉ.
ellārum tamiḻ ilakkiyaṅkaḷ yāvaṟṟiyum kaṟkum tolliyi mēṟkoḷḷa vēṇṭiya tilli, atu muṭiyavum muṭiyātu, tēviyumilli. 
ellā nūlkaḷilum uḷḷa ciṟanta uṇmikaḷi ellām piḻi cāṟāka yāṉ vaṭittut tantuḷḷēṉ.
eṉ nūlkaḷip paṭittāṟpōtum, ataṉāl, tamiḻ muḻutuṅ kaṟṟa payaṉi aṭiyalām.
- maṟimaliyaṭikaḷ



cāṉṟitaḻ
nāki - tiru. vētācalam piḷḷiyavarkaḷ orupōtu tiruvaṉantapurattil oruvāran taṅkiyirunta ñāṉṟu, avaruṭaṉ neruṅkip paḻakum vāyppuk kiṭittataṟkuc cālavum makiḻkiṉṟēm. varuṅkālat tamiḻ makkaḷukku maṭṭaṟṟa tamiḻppulami mikuntu varumeṉpataṟkuc cāṉṟāka ivar iḷamiyilēyē nuṇmāṇ nuḻipulam uṭiyarāyiruppatu kaḷippūṭṭukiṟatu. maṟṟum tāymoḻip paṟṟuk kuṟinta ikkālattil, ivar tamatu nuṇṇaṟivit tamiḻ nūlkaḷil muṟṟum celutti naṉkārāyntuḷḷatum pārāṭṭaṟkuriyatu. pattuppāṭṭu, kalittoki, cīvakacintāmaṇi mutaliya paḻantamiḻ nūlkaḷil ārāycciyuṭaṉ kūṭiya ivartam aṟivuṭimiyē eṉṉi ivarpāl matippuṟumāṟu ceytatu. tamiḻāciriyarkaḷiṉ toki curuṅkivarum ikkālattil ivarukkuc ciṟanta ūkkamaḷittāl ivar oru ciṟanta tamiḻāciriyarāvāreṉṟu nām uṟutiyāka nampukiṉṟēm. muṉṉarē iraṇṭāntarak kallūrikaḷil tamiḻkaṟpikkap pōtumāṉa aḷavu ilakkaṇa ilakkiyattiṟ ciṟanta tērcci peṟṟavarāyirukkiṉṟāreṉak karutukiṉṟōm. Fiwªj msÉš v~¥, V., (F.A.) vakuppukkuriya āṅkila aṟivu peṟṟārāṉāl mēlnāṭṭu muṟippaṭi tamiḻ nūlkaḷi varalāṟṟuc cāṉṟuṭaṉ ārāyavum eḻutavum vallunarāvāreṉpatu emmuṭiya ciṟanta eṇṇam.

2-12-1895
(inta naṟcāṉṟu tirunelvēli intuk kallūriyil cuntaram piḷḷiyavarkaḷ pērāciriyarāka iruntapōtu koṭukkap peṟṟatu.)

maṟimaliyaṭikaḷiṉ mummoḻippulami
ivvirupatām nūṟṟāṇṭil īṭum eṭuppu miṉṟit tikaḻnta māperum pulavar maṟimaliyaṭikaḷ eṉpatu, ellārkkum oppa muṭinta vuṇmiyākum.
ulaka moḻikaḷ (ēṟattā) mūvāyirattuḷ, oru pōtum vaḻaṅkā ilakkiyap perumoḻi yeṉṉum vakiyiṟ camaṟkirutamum, eṉṟumuḷḷa ulakamutal uyar taṉic cemmoḻi yeṉṉum vakiyil tamiḻum, ulakap potuk kalavip perumoḻi yeṉṉum vakiyil āṅkilamum, taliciṟanta moḻikaḷākum. im mūṉṟum oruṅkē kivantār palar iruntārēṉum, avaraṉivaruḷḷum, evarettu (Everest) eṉṉum veḷḷimali pōluyarntum, amēcāṉ (Amazon) eṉṉum amerikka āṟupō lakaṉṟum, amiti vāriyiṉ (Pacific Ocean) teṉṉakaḻi pōlāḻntum, piṟaṅkit tōṉṟiya perum pulami vāyntavar maṟimaliyaṭikaḷē yeṉpatu, mikiyākātu.
- moḻiñāyiṟu ñā. tēvanēyap pāvāṇar
maṟimaliyaṭikaḷ nūṟṟāṇṭu niṉivu malar (pak. 11 - 14)

maṟimaliya māṇpu
nuḻivuri
taṉittamiḻ eṉṟa aḷavil muṉ niṟkum muḻuturu eḻilvaṭivar
tavattiru. maṟimaliyaṭikaḷārē āvar!
munti aṭikaḷ ākiya iḷaṅkōvaṭikaḷ, `muttamiḻ aṭikaḷ eṉa muḻakkamiṭa niṉṟār!
irupatām nūṟṟāṇṭu maṟimaliyaṭikaḷō, taṉittamiḻt tanti eṉṉum takavārnta perumi peṟṟār.
tamiḻ taṉittu iyaṅkātu eṉṟum, vaṭamoḻi vaḻiyatu eṉṟum, vaṭamoḻit tuṇiyiṉṟi iyaṅkātu eṉṟum, ulaka moḻikaḷuk kellām tāymoḻi vaṭamoḻiyē eṉṟum  tamiḻk kalvi valla peṟṟiyarum muṟṟum mayaṅki yurikka,
vaṭamoḻi ulaka moḻikaḷukkut tāymoḻi eṉṟāl, ataṟkum maṟṟi moḻikaḷukkum tanti moḻi tamiḻē eṉṟu kūṟiya vaṭalūr aṭikaḷār pukaḻparappāḷarāyp potunilik kaḻakam kaṇṭa tava aṭikaḷār, taṉittamiḻt tanti eṉṉum perumi peṟṟa maṟimaliyaṭikaḷār āvar.
civaṉiyam cārnta perumakkaḷum, pēraṟivarkaḷum kūṭa, oppārāy, `taṉittamiḻ (taṉittu amiḻ) eṉṟu paḻikkavum iḻikkavum, tuṇintu niṉṟu, avar veḷka; veḷḷiṭi maliyākat `taṉittamiḻ eṉpati nili nāṭṭiya niṟimali, maṟimaliyaṭikaḷārē!
civaṉiya maṭattiṉarum, vaṭamoḻi vallārum, `tol kāppiyak  kaṭal eṉap pukaḻappaṭṭārumām cāmināta tēcikar, nāṇamiṉṟi, 
 ainteḻuttāl orupāṭi eṉṟu aṟiyavē nāṇuvar
  aṟivuṭiyōrē; yāmum atuvē
eṉṟum,
oṉṟē āyiṉum taṉittamiḻ uṇṭō?
eṉṟum - eḻutip parappiṉārāka,
oru col tāṉum vēṟṟuc col viravā nūl yām ceyvēm eṉṟu aimpāṉ nāṉku nūlkaḷ kalimali pēḻiyāy eḻutik kāṭcip paṭuttiya māṭciyar maṟimaliyaṭikaḷār!
tolkāppiyak kāval
tamiḻ moḻikku vāytta tolkāppiya vaḷanūl,
 vaṭacoṟ kiḷavi vaṭaveḻut torīi
  eḻuttoṭu puṇarnta collā kummē	(884)
eṉṟum,
moḻi peyarppuc ceytalilum muṟimi pēṇa vēṇṭum eṉpati,
 moḻipeyarttu atarppaṭa yāttal	(1597)
eṉṟum,
pēccu vaḻakkil oru kāl vēṟṟuccol vantu viṭiṉum,
 marapunili tirital ceyyuṭ killi 	(1590)
eṉṟum - varampāṇi ceytum, ac cevviyal vaḻittaṭam citintum cīraḻintum, cēṟum cakatiyum nāṟum ciṟumiyum kuṟumiyum vaḻivaḻiyē eyta mūvāyiram āṇṭukaḷāy, ayalōr cūḻcciyālum tiṟavōr mayakkālum ēṟpaṭṭa aḻipāṭukaḷi ellām tāmē ōr iyakkamāy iruntu, viḻippum eḻucciyum ūṭṭiyavar maṟimali aṭikaḷē eṉpatāl,
āriyattiṉiṉṟu tamiḻi mīṭṭeṭuppa taṟkāka arum pāṭupaṭṭu ilakkiya ilakkaṇa marapōṭu yāṉ kaṟṟa moḻikaḷ muppatu eṉṉum pāvāṇar,
 mūvā yiramāṇṭu mōtum vaṭamoḻiyāl
 cāvām takiniṉṟa taṇṭamiḻi - mēvāk
 kumarit taṉinilikkuk koṇṭuvanta  appar
 amarar maṟimali yār
eṉap pāṭip paraviṉār. `taṉit tamiḻt tantiyār eṉṟa pārāṭṭum ip pāṭṭiṉ talippil vaḻaṅkiṉār.
 nuṇṇiya nūlpalavum kaṟpatē nōṉpāka
 eṇṇiya vāḻnāḷ elāmaḷittup - paṇṇiya
 centamiḻnūl ovvoṉṟum ceppum kaṭalaṟivi
 pintamiḻ kākkum paṭi
eṉa aṭikaḷ kalvi mēmpāṭṭiyum kalit tiṟap paṭippukaḷiṉ mēmpāṭṭi yum pāvēntar pāratitācaṉār pārāṭṭukiṟār.
paltuṟi āṟṟal
aṭikaḷiṉ paltuṟi āṟṟali 1. pērāciriyar, 2. perum pulavar, 3. pāvalar, 4. ārāycciyāḷar, 5. mummoḻip pulavar, 6. moḻipeyarppāḷar, 7. coṟpoḻi vāḷar, 8. eḻuttāḷar, 9. palkalic celvar, 10. taṉittamiḻt tantiyār eṉṟu pāvāṇar pakuttuk kāṭṭukiṟār.
mēlum avar tam nuṇmāṇ nuḻipulatti
paṉimali mukaṭṭiṉ uyarvum, nīla āṟṟiṉ nīḷamum amiti vāriyiṉ āḻamum amintavar maṟimaliyaṭikaḷ eṉkiṟār. (paṉimali  - imayam; nīla āṟu - nil; amitivāri - pacipikku mākaṭal).
aṭikaḷār nūlkaḷ
aṭikaḷ paṉmāṇ māttamiḻ nūlkaḷi, nūlaka iyakkat tanti araṅka nātarum, innāḷ civacittānta nūṟpatippuk kaḻaka āṭciyarum annāḷ nūlakarumākiya tirumiku irā. muttuk kumāracāmi avarkaḷum 14 vakimippaṭutti vittami perum pārāṭṭukku uriyatām avi,
1. maruttuvam  2. maṟiporuḷ  3. ilakkiyam 4. itaḻkaḷ  5. caṅka  ilakkiyam  6. pāṭal 7. nāṭakam  8. putiṉam 9. kaṭitam 10. kaṭṭuri 11. camayam  12. tattuvam 13. varalāṟu 14. camūka iyal eṉpaṉa.
mutaṟ kuṟaḷ vāta nirākaraṇam eṉṉum maṟuppu nūlē, aṭikaḷiṉ mutal nūlāka veḷippaṭukiṉṟatu (1898) annūṉ maṟuppu nayamē nāki aṭikaḷi - pallavapura aṭikaḷāka -taṉittamiḻ aṭikaḷāka ākka arum perum tūṇṭal āyiṟṟām.
aṭikaḷār varalāṟu: kīḻkaṭal cār nākappaṭṭiṉattic cārnta ciṟṟūr kāṭampāṭi, āṅku vāḻnta aruntavap peruntakiyar cokkanātar - ciṉṉammiyār! ivviṇiyar koṭiyāyt tamiḻaṉṉi ceyta tavap pēṟṟāl piṟantavar vētācalam. piṟanta peru maṅkala nāḷ ki.pi. 15.07.1876.
cokkanātar, cittamalam aṟuvitta cittamaruttuvar; aṟiva maruttuvam eṉpatum atu. attoḻil avarkku vaḷatti vaḻaṅkiyatu. ataṉāl vētācalam kuṟivaṟa vāḻntār.
nākappaṭṭiṉattu veciliyaṉ micaṉ paḷḷiyil aintu vakuppuvari payiṉṟār; ciṟanta māṇavarāka viḷaṅkiṉār. āṟām vakuppil cērum niliyil, arumit tantiyār iyaṟki eytiṉār. āyiṉum aṉṉiyār uṟutiyāl kalviyit toṭarntār. appaḷḷiyil āṅkilak kalvikku irunta mutaṉmi tamiḻukku illi. kiṟittava camayam paṟṟi aṉṟic civaṉiyam paṟṟi aṟiya vāyppilli eṉṉum iraṇṭu kuṟikaḷiyum iḷamiyilēyē uṇarntār.
vīṭṭilēyē tamiḻ nūlkaḷip payiṉṟu mutaṟ kuṟiyiyum, nākiyil irunta intumatāpimāṉa caṅkam vaḻi iraṇṭām kuṟiyiyum nīkkiṉār. paḷḷiyilum kuṟiyaṟak kaṟṟār. varuvāyk kuṟiyāl vāṭum aṉṉikkuc cumiyāy illāmal taṉiyē payilvatē iṉiyavaḻi eṉa eṇṇi, nākiyil puttaka vaṇikarāka viḷaṅkiya nārāyaṇa cāmiyār pulamiyālum ciṟantu viḷaṅkiyavar. ātalāl avariṭam taṉiyē ilakkiya  - ilakkaṇa - camaya nūlkaḷik kaṟṟār. nārāyaṇariṉ muṉṉi māṇavar pērā. maṉōṉmaṇīyam pe. cuntaraṉār eṉpatiyum aṟintār. oṉpatām vakuppoṭu paḷḷik kalviyi niṟuttit taṉikkalviyē kaṟṟār. tolkāppiyam, toki nūlkaḷ, tirukkuṟaḷ, tirucciṟṟampalak kōvi, iṟiyaṉār akap poruḷ eṉakkaṟṟār.
aṭikaḷār iyaṟṟiya tiruvoṟṟi murukar mummaṇik kōvi mukappil, ematu patiṉintām āṇṭil muṟippaṭi tuvaṅkiya tamiḻp payiṟciyāṉatu ematu irupattōrām āṇṭiṟkuḷ tolkāppiyam, tirukkuṟaḷ, ciṟṟampalak kōviyār eṉṉum mūṉṟu nūlkaḷiyum muḻutum neṭṭuruc ceytu muṭittēm eṉṟum vēṟucila nūlkaḷiyum muḻuvatāka ōtiyatiyum kūṟi,
iṅṅaṉamāka, viḻumiya tamiḻppaḻa nūlkaḷil ematu karuttu īrppuṇṭu niṉṟu payiṉṟa payiṟciyiṉālēyē ceyyuḷum uriyum taṉic centamiḻ naṭiyil eḻutum tiṟam ematu iḷamip paruvattilēyē emakku vāyppa tāyiṟṟu eṉkiṟār. avvāṇṭu ki.pi. 1897. nāki intu matāpimāṉa caṅkattiṟkuc civa cittānta caṇṭa marutam eṉṉum virutu peṟṟa cōmacuntara nāyakar vantu coṟpoḻivāṟṟiṉār. avar poḻivil vanta karuttoṉṟaṉi maṟuttu oruvar eḻuta, ataṟku maṟuppāka murukavēḷ eṉṉum puṉipeyaril maṟuppuri eḻutiṉār. ataṉikkaṟṟa nāyakar, mīḷap poḻivukku varuṅkāl muruka vēḷic cantikka virumpiṉār. accantippiṉ payaṉē uṉṉi virivilē ceṉṉikku varuvippōm. nī antap pakkaṅkaḷil iruppatē nallatu eṉṟu kūṟiṉār. ituvē aṭikaḷārkkuc ceṉṉi vāḻvu amiya mutal tūṇṭal āyiṟṟu. aṭikaḷukkup patiṉēḻām vayatil (1893) tirumaṇam nikaḻntatu. tuṇivi tam māmaṉ makaḷ cavuntara valli eṉpār.
tiruvaṉantapuram:
nārāyaṇacāmi avarkaḷiṉ muṉṉi māṇavarām pērā. cuntaraṉār tiruvaṉanta purattil iruntār. avariyaṟṟiya maṉōṉmaṇīya nāṭaka nūl kaṟṟu ataṉ arumi kaṇṭu, pērāciriyarik kāṇavirumpiṉār. ataṉāl tam āciriyarōṭu tiruvaṉantapuram ceṉṟu pērāciriyarōṭu uriyāṭiṉār. vētācalaṉār iḷam paruvattiyum mūtaṟiviyum aṟinta cuntaraṉār viyappuṟṟut takutic cāṉṟu oṉṟu vaḻaṅkiṉār. ciṟitu kālam tiruvaṉanta purattil āciriyap paṇiyum ceytār. avviṭattiṉ paruvanili ovvāmiyāl nākikkē tirumpiṉār.
ceṉṉi: cōmacuntaraṉār muṉṉē kūṟiyapaṭi vētācalaric ceṉṉikku aḻittu aṟiñar nallucāmi eṉpār naṭāttiya cittānta tīpiki eṉṉum itaḻāciriyar ākkiṉār. piṉṉē ceṉṉi kiṟittavak kallūrikku oru tamiḻp pērāciriyar vēṇṭiyiruttal aṟinta cōmacuntaraṉār vētācalaṉā ra akkallūrip pērāciriyarākka muyaṉṟār. atukāl tamiḻttuṟit talivarāka iruntavar vi.kō cūriyanārāyaṇa cāttiriyār eṉṉum paritimāl kaliñar. kallūri mutalvar villiyam millar eṉpār.
9.3.1898 il vētācalaṉār paṇiyēṟṟār. aṅkup patiṉmūṉṟu āṇṭukaḷ paṇiyāṟṟiyuḷḷār.
akkallūrik kālattē iḷaṅkali māṇavarkkup pāṭamāka irunta mullippāṭṭu (1903) paṭṭiṉappāli (1906) eṉpavaṟṟukku ārāycci uri eḻutiṉār.
avvuri nūli vāṅkik kaṟṟa māṇākkarum ēṉit tamiḻaṟiñarum putumuṟiyāka eḻutappaṭṭa avvurip pāṅkiṉip piṉpaṟṟip paḻiya tamiḻ nūlkaḷi ārāyvatil kiḷarcci peṟalāṉār. ipputumuṟi uri, tamiḻ nūlārāyum vakikaḷi iṉitu viḷakki aṟiñar palarkkum aka makiḻcci yiṉi viḷittami kaṇṭu iṉi, ittakiya urikaḷiyē eḻututal vēṇṭum eṉa ematuḷḷam peritum viḻintatu eṉkiṟār.
ceṉṉip palkalik kaḻakattārāṅkilamum vaṭa moḻiyum kaṭṭāyamākap paṭittal vēṇṭum eṉṟum, tāymoḻip pāṭatti viruppap pāṭamākak kaṟpikkalām eṉṟum oru tīrmāṉam āṭcikkuḻuvil koṇṭu vantaṉar. oru pāṭam viruppappāṭam eṉappaṭṭa piṉṉar ataṉik kaṟka varuvār evar? tamiḻ kaṟkum māṇavar eṇṇikki kuṟiyavē āciriyap paṇiyiṭamum kuṟiya vēṇṭuvatāki, aṭikaḷār 31.1. 1911 ām nāḷuṭaṉ kallūrip paṇiyi muṭikka vēṇṭiya tāyiṟṟu.
māṇavar: ikkālattil aṭikaḷiṉ māṇavarāka iruntāruḷ, taṇikimaṇi ceṅkalvarāyar, iracikamaṇi ṭi.kē.ci, nāvalar ca.cō. pārati, pēraṟiñar ca. viyāpuriyār mutalōr cilar.
taṇikimaṇiyiṉ, tēvāra tiruvācakac collaṭivu oru piṟappaḷavil ceyyak kūṭiyatā?
nāvalar ca. cō. pāratiyāriṉ āyvut tuṇivu ettaṉi kacaṭukaḷi nīkkiyatu? viyāpuriyār, kāla āyvilum, vaṭamoḻip piṭivātattilum tammic cālpu āyvil iruntu vilakkiṉālum, caṅka ilakkiyap patippu, nikaṇṭuppatippu, palkalik kaḻaka akaramutalip patippu ivaṟṟic ceyta arumi eṉṉa? puṟattiraṭṭu, kaḷaviyal, kāriki āyavaṟṟiṉ patippuc ciṟappu eṉṉa?
ivarkaḷ paṇiyum aṭikaḷārit taḻuvutal uṇmi tāṉē!
camaraca caṉmārkkam:
kallūrip paṇiyil iruntu vīṭupeṟṟa aṭikaḷār, pallavapuratti vāḻviṭamākak koṇṭār. camaraca caṉmārkka niliyam tōṟṟuvittār. 
ellāc cātiyārkkum ellāc camayattārkkum aṅkīkāramāṉa cīvakāruṇiya oḻukkattiyum, camaraca caṉmārk kattiyum piṟkālattil mika valiyuṟuttip pōtitta āciriyar srīmat irāmaliṅka cuvāmikaḷē āvār. accuvāmi kaḷ valiyuṟuttiya avviraṇṭu koḷkikaḷiyum eṅkum valiyuṟutti viḷakki aṉparkaḷi oruṅku kūṭṭa vēṇṭi accuvāmikaḷ iṭṭa peyarālēyē, `camaraca caṉmārkka niliyam eṉṉum itu tāpikkalāyiṟṟu. inniliyattiṟku muṉ āciriyar tiruvaḷḷuva nāyaṉār; piṉ āciriyar irāmaliṅka cuvāmikaḷ āvar eṉṟu peyarīṭu ceytu koḷkikaḷiyum vakukkiṟār.
kuṭumpam
1898il aṭikaḷār ceṉṉikku varumpōtu avartam aṉṉiyār ciṉṉammiyār, tuṇiviyār cavuntiram ammi yār, celvi cintāmaṇi, aṭikaḷār eṉṉum nālvarik koṇṭa kuṭumpamāka iruntatu.
1911il pallava purattil kuṭipukum pōtu nīlāmpiki, tiruñāṉa campantam, māṇikkavācakam, tirunāvukkaracu, cuntara mūrtti eṉṉum makkaḷum koṇṭa oṉpatiṉmar kuṭumpamāka iruntatu. pallavuram pukunta piṉ tiripura cuntari piṟakkap  patiṉmaṉ kuṭumpam āyiṟṟu. inniliyilēyē 27.08.1911il civat tuṟaviyarkkuriya tuṟavāṭi (kāvi) koṇṭu cuvāmi vētācalam āṉār. cuvāmi vētācalam eṉpataṉ taṉittamiḻākkamē maṟimaliyaṭikaḷ eṉpatu veḷippaṭi. muṉṉēyē avari aṭikaḷ eṉṟatu avartam māṇpu karutiyēyām.
mīḷpārvi:
nām mīḷpārvi oṉṟupārkka vēṇṭum.
aṭikaḷ 21ām akaviyilēyē taṉittamiḻ āyvuttiṟam kivarap peṟṟār.
1903, 1907 ākiya āṇṭukaḷil mullippāṭṭu, paṭṭiṉappāli ākiya nūlkaḷukku āyvuri vaḻaṅkuṅkāl itiluḷḷa coṟkaḷ ittaṉi; ivaṟṟuḷ centamiḻccol ittaṉi; vēṟṟuc col ittaṉi - eṉa aṟutiyiṭukiṟār.
ātalāl, taṉittamiḻ eṉṉum tōṟṟam 1916 ām āṇṭil nīlāmpikiyār tūṇṭalāl ēṟpaṭṭami eṉpatu, akattē amintu kiṭanta vittu, muḷi viṭṭu veḷippaṭṭa niliyām. av veḷippaṭṭa niliyum uṭaṉē paṟṟip paṭaravilli! mēlum cila āṇṭukaḷiṉ piṉṉarēyē camacara caṉmārkka niliyam, potunilik kaḻakamākavum, ñāṉacākaram aṟivuk kaṭalākavum, cuvāmi vētācalam maṟimaliyaṭikaḷ ākavum paṭippaṭiyē kiḷarkiṉṟaṉavām!
ōr ariya amippu uruvākum niliyum, ōr ariya kaṇṭu piṭippu ulakavar aṟiya veḷippaṭalum, avi iyakkamākavum payaṉākavum ākiṉṟa niliyum paṟṟiya viḷakkam, aṭikaḷār taṉittamiḻ iyakka varalāṟṟil aṭaṅkiyuḷḷatām!
etiriṭit tūṇṭal: mēlum oṉṟu: oru nēriṭit tūṇṭaliṉum etiriṭit tūṇṭal valimi mikkatu eṉpatum, uḷḷattu uṟutiyi veḷḷap perukkāy veḷippaṭutta vallatu eṉpatum teḷivām.
oṉṟē āyiṉum taṉittamiḻ uṇṭā? eṉṟu kaṟṟaṟinta tamiḻar oruvarē viṉāvi nakittal, peṟṟa tāy moḻip piṟavikku uṟṟa takimi ākātu eṉpatum, ataṉi uṇarnta appiṟavi pāmpaṉ kurupāta aṭikaḷ pōl cēntaṉ centamiḻ eṉṉa oru nūliyaṟṟit tamiḻ taṉittu iyaṅkavalla taṉi uyar moḻi eṉṟu nilināṭṭiyiruppiṉ vaḻipaṭattakka piṟaviyāy amintirukkum! appēṟu ataṟku vāykkāmi,
tuṟantārkkut tuppuravu vēṇṭi maṟantārkol 
  maṟṟi yavarkaḷ tavam	(kuṟaḷ 263)
eṉṉum vaḷḷuvar vāy moḻippaṭi,
taṉittamiḻt tantip pēṟu maṟimaliyaṭikaṭku vara iruppataṟkē nēriṭṭa nērcciyām eṉka.
iyakkam
aṭikaḷ iyakkam ālamarameṉak kālpala koṇṭu akala akala viriyat taṉittamiḻ iyakkamāy, ulakattamiḻk kaḻakamāy, pāvalarēṟu tamiḻk kaḷamāy, cērar koṟṟamāy, tamiḻkkāppuk kaḻakamāy, tamiḻp pātukāppuk kaḻakamāy, taṉittamiḻ ilakkiyak kaḻakamāy, tamiḻ vaḷarccik kaḻakamāy, maṟimaliyaṭikaḷ maṉṟamāy, tamiḻkkāval kuḻuvāy, iyaṟṟamiḻp payiṟṟakamāy, pāvāṇar tamiḻ iyakkamāy āṅkāṅku mukiḻttu muṉippoṭu kaṭaṉāṟṟa ēviyatām.
nūlakam
aṭikaḷ nūṟṟoki, arumaṇik kuviyaṉṉa nālāyiram nūlkaḷik koṇṭatu. etiratu uṇarntu, civacittānta nūṟpatippuk kaḻakattiṟku muṟkoṭiyāka vaḻaṅkiyatāl, nālāyiram nāṟpatāyiramāy mēlum mēlum virintu maṟimaliyaṭikaḷ nūlakap peyarāl, perumimiku vaḷḷalār mutaṟkaṇ poḻivu ceyta periyavar cōmu avarkaḷ illamē vāykka ulaka vaḷamākkiyatām! ataṉ vaḻiyākavē aṭikaḷ tāmē veḷiyiṭṭa nūlkaḷum, veḷiyiṭāmal kaḻaka vaḻi veḷivanta nūlkaḷum, aṭikaḷ kālattiṟkup piṉ veḷivanta nūlkaḷum ākiya muḻumit tokutikaḷum ki.pi. 1898 mutal 1969 vari mutaṟpatippāka veḷivantavi. avaṟṟuḷ paṭikaḷ kiṭṭutaṟku ariyaviyum uṇṭu. aṭikaḷ nūlkaḷ nāṭṭuṭimi ākkappaṭṭa pēṟṟāl, iṉṟu vāytta nūlkaḷ aṉittiyum oṭṭumottamāka vaḻaṅkum peruvāyppit tamiḻmaṇ patippakam peṟṟatām.
veḷḷac cētam
aṇmiyil ēṟpaṭṭa veḷḷap perukku patippakattuḷ pukuntu accu aṭikkappaṭṭatum, aṭikkappaṭa iruntatum, kaṇiṉippaṭutti vitta kuṟuvaṭṭukaḷum,  mūlanūlkaḷum eṉa vāri aḷḷik koṇṭu pōṉami, kaḷattukku vanta katir maṇikaḷ, kaḷañciyattiṟku vārāmal pōya, pēraḻivu oppatām. ātalāl, nūlkaḷi mīḷat tēṭavum, kaṇiṉippaṭuttavum, kuṟuvaṭṭu ākki acciṭavum eṉa mummaṭaṅkup paṇiyum pēriḻappum perutta avalamum uṇṭākkiṟṟām. anniliyil,
ūḻiyum uppakkam kāṇpar uliviṉṟit
  tāḻā tuñaṟṟu pavar
eṉṉum vāymoḻiyi vāymi moḻiyākkiya tuṇivuc celvar iḷavaḻakaṉārum avar kuṭumpattavarum āvar!
muṉṉaṟivipput tiṭṭappaṭi nūlukku ārvamāyp paṇam celuttiyōrkkum nūl kālattil kiṭṭā uḷiccal illāmal pōkātē! avarkaḷum amintu tāṅkal, iyaṟki iyaṅkiyal vaḻiyatām.
veḷiyīṭukaḷ
tamiḻmaṇ patippakam tamiḻmoḻi iṉa maṇvaḷam - vaḷarcci mīṭṭeṭuppu - eṉpavaṟṟi muṉvittut tamiḻp pōrāḷi iḷavaḻakaṉārāl toṭaṅkappaṭṭatāl, pēriḻappukkām eṉat teḷivākat terintum, icik kalikku vāytta oppaṟṟa kaḷañciyamāy vāytta - karuṇāmirta cākaratti veḷiyiṭṭatām! muṉṉi vanta mutaṟpakuti pēriḻappākkiyum ataṉ piṉṉip pakutikaḷiyum aritiṉ muyaṉṟu veḷiyiṭṭatu! moḻiñāyiṟu pāvāṇar; īḻattu aṟiñar na.ci. kantiyā;  varalāṟṟaṟiñar cāmināta carmā, urivēntar auvi cu.tu; nāvalar na.mu.vē; paṇṭita vittuvāṉ ti.vē. kōpālar; cāmi citamparaṉār; mayili cīṉi. vēṅkaṭa cāmiyār; pēraṟiñar ka. veḷḷi vāraṇaṉār; pāvalar kuḻantiyār; pāvalar muṭiyaracaṉār; pāvēntar, tol poruḷ kalveṭṭu āyñar catācivaṉār, cāttaṉkuḷam irākavaṉ  nūlkaḷiyum eḷiyēṉ nūlkaḷiyum muḻumi muḻumiyāka veḷiyiṭṭatu.
tolkāppiyam- pāṭṭu - toki - kāppiyam - kīḻkkaṇakku - tamiḻnāṭṭu varalāṟu - āṅkilam valla aṟiñar irāma nātaṉār nūlkaḷiyum, yāḻppāṇa akarāti mutalām akarātit tokutikaḷiyum veḷiyiṭṭatu. patiṉeṭṭām nūṟṟāṇṭiṉ piṟpakutitoṭṭu, irupattōrām nūṟṟāṇṭiṉ muṟpakuti vari veḷivanta - tamiḻukku ākkamām nāṭ ṭuṭimi ākkappaṭṭa nūlkaḷi ellām - ōriṭattup peṟṟu, oru taṉittamiḻ nūlakam amikkum aḷavukku acciṭṭu vaḻaṅkiya perumi iḷavaḻakaṉārkku uṇṭu. avarē aṉṟi avar tuṇiyiyum makkaḷiyum uṟaviyum aṉpiyum naṇpiyum oruṅkē iṇittu, aṟiñar perumakkaḷ palar aravaṇippiyum vāyppak koṇṭu vāḻum iyalpu avarkku vāyttalāl maṭṭumē, ivvāṟu ceyaṟkariya ceyya vāyttatām eṉpataṟku eṭuttukkāṭṭākat tikaḻpavarum avar.
maṟimaliya māṇpu
maṟimaliyam, taṉittamiḻ iyakkam! tamiḻt taṉip peruvaḷam! muttamiḻc curappu! cīrtiruttac ceḻumi! vāḻviyal vaḷam! kalic collākkak kaḷañciyam! paṇṭit tamiḻar varalāṟṟup putiyal! āyviyal vaḻikāṭṭi! poḻivup polivu! eḻuttiṉ eḻil! moḻik kāval muṉiyam! muṭṭaṟukkum muḻutuṟu koṭi! mīṭṭeṭukkum mētakimi! aḷḷa aḷḷak kuṟiyā aṇimaṇik kuviyal! eṉal meymi viḷakkamām!
puttakam ēṟṟup polivatē puttakam
 vāḻiya nalaṉē! vāḻiya nilaṉē!
tiruvaḷḷuvar niliyam,	iṉpa aṉpuṭaṉ
7, irāmaṉ teru,	irā. iḷaṅkumaraṉ
tirunakar, maturi - 6
625006.

malikka vikkum maṟimaliyam!
patippuri
moḻiyē iṉamakkaḷ muṉṉēṟa nalla
vaḻiyām ataṉi vaḷarkka -paḻiyāyt
tamiḻayarat tāḻntāṉ tamiḻaṉ avaṉē
tamiḻuyarat tāṉuyarvāṉ tāṉ
moḻippēraṟiñar pāvāṇar avarkaḷ `moḻiyiṉ vaḻiyākavē moḻipēcum makkaḷ muṉṉēṟa muṭiyum eṉak kūṟukiṟār. ulakam muḻutum ivvuṇmi uṇarappaṭṭuḷḷati varalāṟu kāṭṭukiṟatu.
pāvāṇari aṟivulakam cariyāka aṭiyāḷam kāṇpataṟkuk kāraṇamāṉatu maṟimaliyaṭikaḷār tanta pārāṭṭu!
maṟimaliyaṭikaḷ (15.7.1876 - 15.9.1950) tēvanēyap pāvāṇar (7.2.1902 - 16.1.1981) iruvarum taṉittamiḻiyakkattit tāṅki niṟkum tūṇkaḷākap pōṟṟappaṭupavarkaḷ.
maṟimaliyaṭikaḷār 1916il tōṟṟuvitta taṉittamiḻ iyakkam, nūṟṟāṇṭi eṭṭiyuḷḷa makiḻvāṉa nēram itu!
taṉittamiḻiyakka nūṟṟāṇṭi niṉivu kūrum vakiyil, taṉittamiḻiyakkat tanti maṟimaliyaṭikaḷāriṉ eḻuttu, coṟpoḻivu, nāṭkuṟippu, kaṭitaṅkaḷ aṉittiyum `maṟimaliyam eṉum peruntokuppākat tamiḻmaṇ patippakam vaḻi veḷikkoṇarat tiṭṭamiṭṭōm.
ulakam muḻutum paravi vāḻum uṇarvu kuṉṟāta tamiḻarkaḷ tanta ūkkuvippu, intap peruntiṭṭatti niṟivēṟṟit tīravēṇṭum eṉum uṟutiyi eṉakkut tantatu.
taṉittamiḻ iyakka aṟiñarkaḷum ārvalarkaḷum aṭikaḷāriṉ ariya paṭippukaḷ palavaṟṟiyum tēṭittēṭi vaḻaṅki vaḻikāṭṭiṉar; valimiyūṭṭiṉar.
maṟimaliyaṭikaḷār 1898ām āṇṭiliruntu eḻuti vantuḷḷār. aṟivukkaṭal (ñāṉacākaram) itaḻi 1902ām āṇṭiliruntu veḷiyiṭṭu vantuḷḷār. avariṉ paṭippukaḷ peruveḷḷamāy veḷivantirukkiṉṟaṉa.
accēṟātavi, peyar kūṭak kēṭṭaṟiyātavi, peyar terintum pārvikkuk kiṭṭātavi - eṉap palavakiyāyk kiṭittaṉa aṭikaḷāriṉ arumpaṭippukaḷ! avaṟṟi `maṟimaliyam eṉum talippil 34 tokuppukaḷāka orucērap pārkkumpōtu, tamiḻulakam viyappaṭivatu uṟuti.
moḻinalam iṉanalam pēṇutal aṉṟō
muḻunalam peṟuvaḻi! nāmpeṟal eṉṟō?
ippaṭik kavaliyōṭu kēṭpār pāvalarēṟu peruñ cittiraṉār (10.3.1933 - 11.6.1995). avariṉ `teṉmoḻi itaḻ 1.8.1959 iliruntu veḷivarat toṭaṅkiya piṉputāṉ, taṉittamiḻt tici nōkki nūṟṟukkaṇakkāṉōr naṭipōṭat toṭaṅkiṉar. atil nāṉum oruvaṉ.
peruñcittiraṉāriṉ eḻuttu kāṭṭiya aṭiyāḷattāl, pāvāṇar nūlkaḷ eṉum aṟivupputiyal palar kikaḷilum kuṭiyēṟiyatu.
pāvāṇar nūlkaḷ valiyuṟuttip pēciyatāl, maṟimali yaṭikaḷārit tēṭippayilum ārvam tamiḻulakil puttuyir peṟṟatu.
āriyatti etirppatilum camaṟkirutatti vilakku vatilumē tamiḻiṉa mēmpāṭum tamiḻk kāppum aṭaṅkiyirukkiṟatu. avvuṇarcciyit tamiḻar neñcaṅkaḷil arumpa vittatu tirāviṭar iyakkam; malarntu viriyavittatu taṉittamiḻ iyakkam! kumuka, araciyal, paṇpāṭṭu malarccikku inta īriyakkaṅkaḷiṉ pēruḻippup payaṉpaṭṭatu.
tamiḻakattil taṉittamiḻ uṇarvu ki.mu. 7ām nūṟṟāṇṭukkup piṉ taḷarat toṭaṅkiyatu; 10ām nūṟṟāṇṭukkup piṉ cariyat toṭaṅkiyatu. vaṭamoḻi veḷḷattil tamiḻ iḻuttuc cellappaṭṭu viṭāmal kāppāṟṟiyōr kamparum pavaṇantiyum! piṉvantōrukku antak kāppuṇarcci illāmal, ayaṉ moḻip paṟṟukku aṭimiyāṉatāl tamiḻum tamiḻiṉamum peṟṟa iḻappukaḷ ērāḷam!
ayaṉmoḻic cēṟṟiliruntu tamiḻi mīṭkum muyaṟci āṅkilēyar varukiyiyoṭṭip puttuyir peṟṟatu. vaḷḷalār, pāmpaṉ cāmikaḷ, vaṇṇac carapam taṇṭapāṇi cuvāmikaḷ, cuppiramaṇiyacivā, paritimāṟ kaliñar mutaliya palarum toṭakka vitikaḷāyiṉar.
kōvi avināciyil piṟantu virutunakaril vāḻnta viruti civañāṉayōki 19.11.1908 ām nāḷ `tiruviṭar kaḻakam toṭaṅkit taṉittamiḻ uṇarccikku vēkaṅkūṭṭiṉār. avvamippil uṟuppiṉarāyiruntavar cuvāmi vētācalam (malimaliyaṭikaḷ).
maṟimaliyaṭikaḷ eṉat tam peyarik koṇṭu, 1916il avar toṭaṅkiyatē taṉittamiḻ iyakkam.
`tuṟimiku āṅkilam maṟivaṭa moḻiyoṭu
 tūytamiḻp pēraṟiñar - taṉittamiḻ
 tōṟṟiya ōraṟiñar
pāvalarēṟu peruñcittiraṉār pāṭiyataṟkēṟpa, maṟimali yaṭikaḷār tamiḻ, āṅkilam, camaṟkirutam mutaliya paṉmoḻiyilum pulami mikkavar.
paṉmoḻip pēraṟiñarāṉa maṟimaliyaṭikaḷār tamiḻukkum tamiḻarukkum ātaravāka niṉṟatāl etirppukkum iḻappukkum āḷākkappaṭṭār. āṉālum uṟuti kuṉṟāmal tamiḻukku ātaravāy uḻikkum uḷḷattōṭiruntār. ataṉāl, tamiḻar uḷḷaṅkaḷil iṉṟaḷavum māṟāp perumiyōṭu avar vīṟṟirukkiṉṟār.
 vāḻttāta nāḷilli viyakam
  maṟimali yaṭikaḷ maṟiyāt tiruppeyar
  vāḻttāta nāḷilli viyakam
pāvēntar pāratitācaṉ avar māṇpiṉip pāṭi makiḻntatum, tam `kuṭumpa viḷakku nūliṉi maṟimali yaṭikaḷukkuk kāṇikkiyākki vaḻaṅkiyatum, avariṉ uṟuti taḷarāta uḷḷamum uḻippum kaṇṭu pōṟṟiya naṉṟik kaṭaṉ eṉpatil ayyamilli.
ceṉṉiyil kaṭanta irupatu āṇṭukaḷākac ceyalpaṭṭu varum tamiḻmaṇ patippakap paṇikaḷit tamiḻulakam aṟiyum.
vāḻum talimuṟikkum varuṅkālat talimuṟikkum vaḻikāṭṭavalla tamiḻaṟiñarkaḷ palariṉ nūlkaḷi muḻumuḻut tokuppukaḷāka vaḻaṅkiyiruppatu tamiḻmaṇ patippakattiṉ muṭippiṉi (cātaṉi)yākap pōṟṟappaṭukiṟatu.
tamiḻ maṟumalarccikku vittiṭṭa aṟiñarkaḷik kaṇṭaṟintu, avarkaḷiṉ nūlkaḷi muḻumiyāṉa tokuppukaḷākat tamiḻmaṇ patippakam veḷiyiṭṭu varukiṟatu. avi kālam muḻutum payaṉpaṭavalla karuvi nūlkaḷ eṉpati aṟiñar ulakam aṟiyum!
tamatu nalatti otukki vittu, tamiḻ nalattik kākka vāḻnta aṟiñarkaḷiṉ aṉittu nūlkaḷiyum oru cērat tokuttu muḻut tokuppāka vaḻaṅkuvatu perumpaṇi! ataṉit tamiḻmaṇ patippakam toṭarntu niṟivēṟṟi varukiṟatu. tokuppu nūlkaḷiṉ paṭṭiyalip piṉṉiṇippil kāṇka.
tamiḻukkāka uḻikkum uṇarvi iḷamiyil emmiṭam ēṟpaṭuttiyōruḷ perumpulavar nakkīraṉārum avartam iḷaval pulavar cittiravēlaṉārum kuṟippiṭattakkōr! em naṉṟikkuriyōr!
taṉittamiḻ ārvam, tirāviṭar iyakka īṭupāṭu eṉum iru ciṟakukaḷāl emmi eḻavittavar perumpulavar nakkīraṉār! avari eṇṇik kaṇpaṉikka naṉṟi kūṟukiṟatu em neñcam.
kālappaḻamiyālum parāmarippuk kuṟivālum aḻiya nērnta tamiḻilakkiyaṅkaḷ aḷavaṟṟavi! avaṟṟil orupakutiyi yāvatu mīṭṭu nam kikaḷukku vaḻaṅkiyōr āṟumukanāvalar, ci.vi. tāmōtaraṉār, u.vē.cāminātar mutaliya cāṉṟōr perumakkaḷ!
tamiḻilakkiyap peruñcelvatti mīṇṭum mīṇṭum accākki, aṉivar kikaḷiyum eṭṭac ceyta perumi - civacittānta nūṟpatippuk kaḻakam, u.vē.cā. nūlakam, maṇivācakar patippakam mutaliya patippāḷarkaḷukku uriyatu.
accuttuṟi aṟimukamāṉa piṉpē, aṟivuttuṟi aṉivarukkum potuccottākum vāyppu vantatu. eṭṭāk kaṉiyāka ōlic cuvaṭikaḷil muṭaṅkik kiṭanta ilakkiyac celvaṅkaḷ, ellōrukkum eḷitāyk kiṭikkum paṇiyic ceyta patippāciriyarkaḷum, patippāḷarkaḷum nam naṉṟik kuriyōr!
iravu pakal pārātu mutumiyik karutātu uḻikkum maṉavuratti muṉṉōṭip patippāciriyarkaḷiṉ paṇiyē emakku vaḻaṅkiyatu.
aracum, palkalik kaḻakaṅkaḷum, aṟaniṟuvaṉaṅkaḷum, camaya pīṭaṅkaḷum ,perum iyakkaṅkaḷum, perum celvarkaḷum ceyya vēṇṭiya perun tamiḻppaṇi itu! eḷiyavarkaḷāl iyaṅkum tamiḻmaṇ patippakam peruntamiḻp paṇikaḷi iṉṉalkaḷukku iṭiyil arumuyaṟciyōṭu ceytu varuvatu, uḷḷavuram oṉṟiṉāltāṉ!
vallami cērkkum valimiyuṇ ṭākkum!
  vaṇṭamiḻ nintiṭil etunammik kākkum?
kākkum tamiḻik kākkum uṇarvip pāvēntar pāṭal varikaḷ tantaṉa.
accukku eṭṭāmalum kikkuk kiṭṭāmalum irunta pēraṟiñar palariṉ nūlkaḷi nāṭṭuṭimiyākki tirāviṭar iyakka aracukaḷ vaḻaṅkiṉa. ataṉāl, aṟivu paravalākum vāyppukaḷ uruvāyiṉa.

āṟṟiyuḷḷa potuppaṇikaḷ
1965il paḷḷi māṇavaṉāka iruntapōtē moḻip pōrāṭṭattil muṉippākap paṅkēṟṟut taḷippaṭuttap peṟṟu 48 nāḷkaḷ ciṟiyil iruntavar.
piṟanta ūrākiya uṟantirāyaṉkuṭikkāṭṭil `ūrnalaṉ vaḷarccik kaḻakam eṉum camūka amippil iruntu ūr nalappaṇi āṟṟiyavar.
urattanāṭṭil `tamiḻar urimik kaḻakam eṉṉum amippiyum, pāvāṇar paṭippakattiyum naṇparkaḷuṭaṉ iṇintu niṟuvit tamiḻmoḻi, tamiḻiṉa, tamiḻaka mēmpāṭṭiṟku uḻittavar. iḷam talimuṟikkut tamiḻt toṇṭāṟṟiyavar.
pēraṟiñar aṇṇāviṉ matuvilakkuk koḷkiyi neñcil ēnti urattanāṭu matuvilakkuk kuḻuviṉ mukkiya amippāḷarkaḷil oruvarāka iruntu ceyalāṟṟiyavar. 1975-il tamiḻnāṭu caṭṭamaṉṟattil `urattanāṭu tiṭṭam eṉṟu pārāṭṭap peṟṟa matuvilakkut tiṭṭam veṟṟi peṟa uḻittavar.
tamiḻmaṇ patippakatti niṟuvi puḻakkattil illāta  paḻantamiḻ nūlkaḷiyum, putiya paṭippu ilakkiyaṅkaḷiyum, 19  ām nūṟṟāṇṭiṉ iṟutiyilum 20ām nūṟṟāṇṭut toṭakkat tilum vāḻnta tamiḻccāṉṟōrkaḷ eḻuti vittuc ceṉṟa tamiḻiṉ aṟivuc celvaṅkaḷit tēṭi eṭuttu muḻumiyākac cērttu poruḷvaḻip pirittu, kāla niralil orē vīccil toṭar toṭarāka  veḷiyiṭṭut tamiḻnūl patippil taṉi muttiri patittu varupavar.

innūlip paṟṟiya kuṟippuri..
māṇikkavācakar tēvāra mūvarkku muṉṉavar eṉṟum, piṉṉavar eṉṟum iruvēpaṟu āyvāḷarkaḷ uḷar. māṇikka vācakar colnaṭi, caṅka naṭicārntatātaliṉ mūvarkku muṉṉavar eṉṟum, cuntarar pāṭiya tirut toṇṭat tokiyil māṇikkavācakar peyar kuṟippiṭappaṭāmiyāl avarkkup piṉṉavar eṉṟum irucārārum kūṟuvar. iccikkali akaṟṟum uṟutiyāl, aṭikaḷār āḻa māka ārāyntu māṇikkavācakar kālam ki.pi. mūṉṟām  nūṟṟāṇṭē eṉṟu muṭivukaṭṭiṉār.
māṇikkavācakar varalāṟu purāṇa nūlkaḷil vevvēṟākak kāṇappaṭuvatāl avaṟṟi uṇmiyeṉak koḷḷāmal tiruvācakam tirukkōvi nūlkaḷil kāṇappaṭum kuṟippu kaḷiyē varalāṟṟuc cāṉṟākak koḷḷa vēṇṭum eṉṉum tērnta muṭivil āyntu varinta perunūl iḵtākum.

PREFACE
The Tamil Work on St. manickavachakar’s life and Times:’ which appewars in th following pages, is the fruit of six years:’ arduous labour almost unremitting except on a few occasions when it had to be lid aside for a short period of a month and a half each time, in order to make room for the preparation of smaller works that demanded my exclusive attention on such occasions. The work war begun on the 16th of August, 1992 and completed only on the 5th of September, 1928.
During this long interval, the first call came to me from the Secretary of the All - india Oriental Conference for the contribution of a paper in English on the system of Saiva Siddhanta, to be read at its sittings that were to commence then in madras on the 23rd of Decenber. 1924. In Response to the call, I prepared a paper in English on ‘The Conception of God as Rudra’ but at the end of a month and a half when the work had progressed to three - fourths of the whole, the secretary pressed me for the paper. I, therefore, sent a copy of it so far writtern and after the emergency was over, left it incomplete and returned to this my Tamil work, for its subject - matter was ever present in my mind and interested me more than anything else.
Agin in the first quarter of the year 1926, many of my readers and friends repeatedly expressed their wish for a second edition of my treatish on ‘caste and its Evil’ which was long out of print. And in compliance with their wish I had to leave the present work for time and direct my attention to rewriting the book on ‘caste and its Evil’. To keep it abreast of the times, much new and additional matter was introduced into it, and the book grew four times in size of its first edition and appeared in June, 1926, in its full and final form.
Agin, from the time my Tamil work on : ‘Personal magnetism:’ began to appear part by part in my magazine: ‘Unansagaram’, demands for it in a complete book - form increased som much that towards the end of 1926. I found it no longer possible to delay its publication. and so, I took up that most Useful work, completed and published it in book - form in February, 1927.
In the middle of 1923, a hot controversy was opened in the Tamil papers on the subject whether the Velala people should be included in the Vaisya, the mercantile caste or in the Sudra, the slave caste of the Aryan classification. I was called upon to decide this moot point, and consequently a critical and historical study of that interesting question was made on the basis of facts gathered from Tamil and Sanscrit Literatures. I traced the origin and growth, the rank and station of the velala community and the results were published in a book-form in Nov., 1923. The copies of the first edition having sold off in three years and half, the second edition was called for early in 1927, and was, with a few more additions, published in july, 1927.
Besides these interruptions cused by literary works, many a public function, which I was called upon to perform, delayed the present work still more, Every now and then I had to go to Perur, thanjavur, Tiruchi, Madras and Thiruppathirippuliyoor for presiding over the grand annual celebrations and Thiruppathirippuliyoor for presiding over the grand annual celebrations of Tamil sangams and Saiva Siddhanta Societies that were held in those places.
In the midst of Such varied activities, I was also for a time striving my utmost to print my English lecture on ‘Saiva Siddhanta as a philosophy of practical knowledge’, but the printing of it could not be finished as yet, since some of its parts require expansion which I can do only after my present literary works leave my hands.
In addition to being the author and publisher, I have to be the printer of my own writings. This cuses me no little trouble and allows me not even sufficient time for taking necessary rest.
With all these troubles, difficulties and frequent interruptions, it gives me no samll pleasure to think that was enable by the grace of Lord Siva to complete this most important work of mine ofter so long a time.
This work comprises two parts. The first part treats of the life of St. Manickavachakar and the second of the times in which he lived. The method pursued in dealing with the two subjects would seem to be quite novel to the present day Tamilians who, having, for the last four or five centuries, come under the influence of the Aryan priesthoond, have lost their independent and rational way of thinking and have become slaves to the Aryan laws,  customs and manners. They now remin hopelessly unprogressive and inimical to all kinds of salutary reforms. Their observances of rites, of reliigious practices and social customs are formal and inflesible, for they care little to understand the significance of what they so strictly but unwittingly obseve. This slavishness, this petrified concervatism has so thickened the gloom of their ignorance as to render them throughly impervious to the ray of light coming from the critical and historcal spirit of the modern culture. Even the few who have acquired and extensive knowledge of Tamil grammar and modern Tamil literature, are unable to recongnize the great value and importance of the critical and historical methods of inquiry, for a clear comprehension of the subject they have taken to study. This is due partly ot their want of acquaintance with the pure subject matter of the ancient Tamil classics which depict nature and human nature as they truly appear, and partly also to their indiscriminate understanding of the later Puranic literature which contins nothing but exaggrated and distorted accounts of nations conflicts as well as the mythical and legendary histories of gods and goddesses. These Puranic stories being quite foreign to the Tamil genius which keeps itself strictly close to nature, are either things imported into Tamil from Sanscrit narratives. In this state of our Pandits:’ knowledge, it is no wonder that my way of treating the history of our St. manickavachakar should appear novel, nay, even strange to their mind.
But it is most gratifying to note that, within the last three decades, from the time I began the publication of my Tamil magazine Jnanasagaram in which for the first time appeared not only my essays on comparative religion, science of language and literary criticism, but also historical studies of ancient authors and Tamil translations of some notable works in Sanscrit and English, a new inquiring spirithas been kindled perceptibly in the minds of educated young men but imperceptibly in the aged Tamil scholars, in a way that it has forcibly opened the eyes of the latter to recognise the merit of this kind of study to and appreciable extent. At the present time there are many here and there who can understand and admire works of this kind, although those who can produce the like of which are still few and far between.
The fact, however, cannot be denied that,. during the past ten or fifteen years, a few books in English and a very few in Tamil purporting to deal with the history of the Tamil language and literature did come out; but it is deplorable that many of them bear no marks of originality nor do they indicate that their authors possessed much first - hand knowledge of the sources from which they pretend to have drawn the materials for their works.
As the only exception to this charge might be cited an earlier ENglish work, called ‘The Tamils Eighteen Hundred Years  Ago’ but it treats of the Tamils that lived before the third century A.D. and leaves out of account the important period - important from a religious point of view, which intervened between the third and the twelfth century A.D., and which th language, the religion, the literature and the social conditions of the Tamils had under gone a marked change by the introduction of the Buddhist, the Jain, and the Brahmanical religions into this country.
This defect might have been removed by a later English work called ‘Tamil studies’ in which the treatment of this period though not full, in correct in many respects; by sadly the merit of it has been detracted by the brahmanical bias and hughtiness which drove the author most unjustifably to stigmatize the whole Tamil people as liars and extol the whole of brahmins as the only truth - telles. Further, the author’s mind is prepossessed with a strong desire to attribute the civilization of the Tamils solely to the influence of  the Aryans that migrated to the south. For a true, impartial, and sympathietic study of an ancient civilized race as the Tamils, I need hardly say that souch a biased state of mind is quite unfit, In the body of this work, I Have gone fully into a discussion of such malicious views and opinions expressed in ‘Tamil Studies’ and have exposed their fallacy and shallow reasoning, by drawing parallel pictures of the Tamils’s and Aryan’s ethical and religious lives. This is done either by quoting my authorities from the literatures of the tow languagesor by referring to the chapter and verse of the works in which they occur and in this way the contrase that have existed from early times between the moral and religious life of the one race and that of the other is set forth, I believe, as clearly as possible.
From such unjust and uncharitable estimates of a nation’s life and gross racial prejudices aginst a highly civilized race, Dr.Krishnaswamy Iyengar’s Ancient India,’ and ‘Some Contributions of South India to Indian Culture’ may be regarded as free to a large extent, though not in toto. For, in these too is visible the tendency to attribute to Aryan influence whatever is excellent in the ancient and modern Tamil classics. Of couse, the tendency of the author might have been due either to his brahminicals caste-prejudice or simply to inadvertence to take congnizance of the schemes and methods of government and of the social, moral, philosophical and religious ideas that were common in olden times to the ruling powers and thinkers, both in the southern and the northern parts of India. There are clear evidences to prove that alomost all the kings who ruled over India in the past, were of the Tamilian stock, whom the Aryan priests had called the kshattriyas. should the same be found also in the Artha Sastra of Kautilya in Sanscrit, one bearing a striking resemblance to the other, anable historian as Dr. Krishnaswamy would not be justified, as he has done in his ‘contributions’,1 in concluding from that and that alone that Thiruvalluvar borrowed his konwledge of it from Kautilya. You must show several other coincidences, not one or thow stray points of similarities as in the present case, before you forgotten that ancient writers on politics were in a large measure recording in their works not their won ideas with regard to what ought to be the principles of an administrative system and how they have to be carried into execution, but only the ideas they had obsered to previl already among the kings and to guide their ruling operations. Unfortunately this has been overlooked by Dr. Krishnaswamy when, in his eagerness to establish the influence of the Aryan writers over the Tamilian, he had lighted on a passage in the sacred Kural of St. Thiruvalluvar which makes reference to a state-craft, resembling that in the Artha Sastra of Kautliya.
The root-cuse of souch a misapprehension even on the part of great scholars who have imbibed the ideas of refined western education, lies deep in the high opinion which one froms of himself, and of his caste at the expense of others and other castes, It is a Iamentable fact that, in spite of their extreme reluctance of mingle with the brahmins of the north, the South Indian brahmins identify themselves with the Tamils. Still, there is not a shadow of proof to establish their clim of descent from the Aryans of the north. Probably they imagine that their caste - superiority can be mintined only by keeping themselves entirely aloof from the people of this country and by climing only Sanscrit as their own language which, being dead and therefore little understood by the people, serves their purpose to make it the language of the celestials and the exclusive repository of revealed scriptures. Further, they repudiate the very idea that any one, not born brahminï even though his mind be imbued with the culturw of Europeanlearning, is scarcely free from this feeling of his caste-supremacy. His mind has becom so saturated with it that it has become part and parcel of his being. He cannot, therefore, brook the thought that anything excellent or original can exist outside the pale of the brahmin caste and brahmin literature. This Extremely self-conceited state of the brahmin mind has so frequently and so prominently shown itself in almost all branches of Sanscrit literature that it has not escaped even the admiring eyes of some great oriental scholars. Forced by its undue prominence, they have not disdined to a vow their disapproval of it. To quate one instance: Zenide Ragozin onserves: “At a later period, the followers of Vasistha and his descendants represent the narrowly orthodos brahmanic school with its petty punctiliousness in the matter of forms, rites, observances, its intolerance of everything un-Aryan, its regid seperatism.”1 It is no wonder then that Dr. Krishnaswamy should attempt, of course without success, to make the divine sage Thiruvalluvar, the most original of Tamil poets and ethical philosophers and the reputed author of the Sacred Kural, the crestjewel of Tamil Classics, a debtor to Kautilya whose existence prior to the first century A.D., the age of Thiruvalluvar, is still in dispute.1
Apart from such flaws - fortunately they are very few, the writing of Dr.Krishnaswamy dealing with the history of Tamil kings and poets, on the whole, a reliable source of valuable information, although the dates assigned by him to be Vaishnava Alwars are incorrect as is shown in the following work of mind.
Coming now to a few Tamil books and magazine articles that have appeared within the last one or two decades, treating of the history of Tamil literature, I find only two books which require special mention here, for in the whole range of the following work, no other views that what were advocated in the two, have had to be taken for a searching criticism and an elaborate discussion, In the course of his perusal, the reader cannot fail to notice the necessity which impels me to meet at every turn the arguments set forth in both the works. Of these two works, one is ‘A History of Tamil Literature’ by Mr.K.Srinivasa Pilli and the other is ‘The life of Seran Senguttuvan’by Pandit M.Raghava Iyengar.
The first is replete with errors resulting from guesses and conjectures and from twisted interpretations but by the verses quoted in support of his views. Apart from the matter taken out of the excellent exegetical editions of  Tamil Classics brought out by the veteran Tamil scholar Pandit V.Saminatha Iyer and some others, what constitues the original contrubutions of the author is, I am very sorry to say, mostly erroneous. Had such matter been presented at least in a style as artistic and as fascinating as that in the historical writings of  J.A.Froude, the reader would have been benifited to a degree. Even such a compensating virtue cannot be met with in this ‘History of Tamil Literature.’ Its Tamil style is impure, commonplace, insipid and ungrammatical, while the matter is mostly incorrect. The subject - matter of my book, being necessarily related to all that is treated in this book, it has become indispensable for me to expose the errors lurking in it and give a true and connected account of almost all the standard Tamil works that preceded and succeeded the time of St. Manickavachakar. For Manickavachakar lived at a time that stands midway between the classical and mediaeval periods of Tamil literature and one who inquires into the characteristic features of his age and its literary productions cannot do full justic to his subject without comparing them with the nature and conditions of the works that were produced both before and after his time.
Now, the second treatise not only relates the life of the powerful Sera monarch Senguttuvan, but also attempts to fix his age, as well as the ages of the classical epics the Silappadhikaram and the Manimekhali, of the prose commentary of Nakkirar on the Tamil poetics called Iriyanar Ahapporul, and of the lyrics composed by the poet Mamoolanar. Thea biographical portion of the book is admirable in every way, whereas the historical is grounded minly on bare suppositions, wild conjectures and on wrong interpretations of verse-quotations. If a student who has familiarised himself with the classical Tamil poems and the old exegetical prose writings, would take the trouble to inquire into the conditions of time that called them into being, he cannot but be struck with the author’s fruitless endeavour to reduce the antiquity of the above mentioned calssics to a comparatively later age. The brahmins, as a whole, hold that there cannot exist in any language, except in their own Sanscrit, any valuable intellectual product that can clim high antiquity. Should any irrefutable facts be foruthcoming to prove the contrary, they evince a pronounced tendency to twist their meaning and character so as to render them conformable to their own favourite views and opinions. This tendencey of the brahmins to distort the meanings of the original text that contin matter unpalatable to them may best be seen in the commentaries written skillfully by their forefathers on several old works in antique Sanscrit. In the present instance the same is what we fine to be the case with the autor of the treatise calles ‘Seran Senguttuvan.’ In the following work I have reviewed one by one all the facts he brought in proof of his views, and have considered them in the light of the indisputable evidence furnished by Tamil and Sanscrit literatures and by epigraphical records, to see whether they lend any support to his theories. The result is that my conclusions have reached a position diametrically opposed to those he arrived at in his treatis ‘Seran Senguttuvan’, I have placed before the reader all my evidence drawn from a first - hand knowledge of the original sources, with the help of which he can himself test the points of defference between us and can easily distinguish what is true from what is untrue.
Thus the two treatise and several others that had equally clamied my attention in the matter of correctly fixing the time of St. Manickavachakar, have had to be unavoidably subjected to a searching criticism and analysis, simply for the sake of truth.
My first attention to the subject war awakened in this wise: Twenty seven years ago when Dr. G.U. Pope’s excellent and scholarly English translation of  Thiruvachakam made its appearance, the learned translator’s opinion as regards the date of St. Manickavachakar,1 gave a strong impulse to an investigation of the views held on the subject by Tamil Pandits and by same English scholar who possessed also a knowledge of Tamil. Between the first and the ninth century A.D.did the opinions of these two distinct classes of learned people oscillate for the age of our Saint. At that time I was running the first volume of my Tamil periodical ‘Jnanasagaram’, and the question of our Saint’s age, being then discussed everywhere in educated circles, drew my attention to it and kindled my interest so much that I Immediately made a careful study of it and published the results in an article contrubuted to that organ. This article was also translated into English by my student Mr.F.T.Peters, B.A. (then in the madras christian college, now Deputy Postmaster General) and was published in ‘The Christian College, Magazine’ for 1904. A Little later the epigraphist Mr.Gopinatah Rao, M.A. contersted my arguments and published his views on the same subject in the same magazine. And a rejoinder to it was writtern by me in my Jnanasagaram for 1908 and with that the controversy about the age of St. Manickavachakar was set at rest. But after a long time it was raked up by Mr.Srinivasa Pilli in his ‘History of Tamil Literature’. Ofcourse for the deification of Tamil students, the same subject may be brought up for discussion any number of times, I am far from objecting to it but every time that it is thus brought up, it must be made to gin ever so much greater light and show the way to a true historcal knowledge based on a sound co-ordinion of literary and epigraphical evidence. On no account must the writer of a historcial treatise allow himself to be carried away by conjectures and fancied theories about literal facts so as to live in a sphere unreal but agreeable to his own favourite purposes but must restrin himself in order that the facts themselves may make room for the emergence of truth and help it live there. But Sadly the author of the work in question betrays a want of this sound historcial sense. For a just and adequate treatment, the history of Tamil literature still awaits the hand of a Max Muller or a Weber, a Macdonell, or a Ragozin. In the folliwing work, I have, to the best of my ability, surveyed the whole extent of Tamil literature beginning with the Tholkappiam the age of which goes back to 3500 B.C., and ending with the Sivajanabodham of the 12th centuray A.D. For the time fo St. Manickavachakar stand midway between the close of the ancient pure classical Tamil period and the beginning of a some-what mixed kind of  literary Tamil period, and cannot be adequately treated of without entering fully into the cuses that led to the formation of wholesome literature and into those that introduced extraneous matter into its system and made way for its decay. I Believe I have not passed over any point of importance in fixing the time of several literary and religious strata and if any one will have the kindness to point out omission of any important point, I shall be very thankfull to him and shall not til to notice it in the second edition.
I have to say a work concerning the way in which the life of our Saint is treated here. Of the verse compositions that narrate the life of our Saint, only four have been taken for a comparative study of his life. Of these four, The Thiruviliyadal Puranam by Perumapatrappuliyoor Nambi is the earliest, being, as has been shown by its learned editor Pandit Saminatha Iyer, composed in the eleventh century A.D. The author of this treatise seems to have had a fair historical sense, since many of his accounts, I find to my great astonishment, are reliable. Next in point of time comes ‘The Thiruviliyadal Puranam’ by Paranjoti, a contemporary of the king Ati Vira Rama Pandian whose reign began between 1562 and 1563 A.D. (see Dr.Krishnaswami Iyengar’s Ancient India, p.375). The author of this work being much influenced by the Sanscrit Purana, gives not only a false colouring even to historical incidents but introduces also many things new and unhistorical and unuthorized by previous works. And the third one is ‘The Thiruvathavurar Puranam’ written by Kadavulmamunivar at the beginning of the19th century A.D. Unlike the above two this is devoted exclusively to a graphic account of the life of our Saint; and this for the most part of our Saint’s life which is touched but very briefly and incompletely in the other. And the last one ‘The Thirupperunthuri Puranam’ by meenakshisundaram Pilli, was writtern in the latter half of the 19th century and possesses little or no historical value for our purpose. Though the four treatises agree in giving a general description of his life as a whole, points of differences in detils are many and numerous among them. wherever it seemed useful and necessary to note the differences between one narrative and the other, I have not only shown them but also argued for and aginst the one or the other and chose what was possible and reasonable under the circumstances pertining to our Saint’s life. In many a place I have quoted the very words of our Saint to make clear why I have chosen one account in preference  to another. For fortunately for us and for the whole religious world, the sacred syrical utterances themselves of our Saint are interspersed with number of incidental references to all the important events to his life, so that a careful student who studies them with a critical understanding cannot but notice the points which, if picked up and strung together, will constitute a coherent and genuine autobiography of a great soul that was God face to face and attined spiritual perfection. in the matter of studying the lives of such great religious founders as Gautama Buddha, Jesus Christ, Mahomed and others, it is not given for us to rely upon the words uttered by the teachers themselves - for they were irretrievably lost unrecorded, but are left to look up to the accounts given by interested persons who came long after them. The description of a rose-flower by a poet, however glowing it may be cannot set before us the exquisite beauty of its form and its colour and the sweetness of its perfume, as the flower itself can directly do. so too the accounts given of a great sint by others, even though they be his intimates, can never draw a true picture of his real self, nor can express the inner workings of his heart as faithfully as he himself can do. Nothing in the world is more mysterious than the ways of a sintly person who struggled through the experience of this lower world to that of a higher one which transcends the knowledge of ordinary mortals. Unless a man can reach the summit of self-realization attined by that great soul, he cannot hope to place himself in a standpoint different from his own, and view the extent as well as profound depth of that great mind. For a true comprehension, therefore, of the essential nature of sanctified souls, the history written of them by others who stand not only much below their level but also far from their time, cannot help us much. If we be so fortunate as to possess the very utterances of such holy men, as in the case of St. Manickavachakar and other great Saiva Saints, we may with the necessary equipment, venture to enter into the sacred precincits of their life and hope to obtin the inexhustible treasures of spiritual experience they so surely acquired within so short a time.
The glory of Tamil literature consists in its possession of such spiritual legacies as have come down to us directly from our great Saints and Sages as Manickavachakar, Appar, Thirujnanasambandar, Sundarar and Maikandadevar. The like of which, we are sorry to say, have not been bequeathed to posterity by any other sint or religious founder in any language, literature, of religion so far as our knowledge goes. The value of a literary production that has come from a grea soul who saw God in a visible form and described Him in a way that makes it easier for us all to bring his image before our mental eye, cannot be rated too highly. One can estimate the value of our Saint’s utterances only by bringing before his mind the great and irreparable loss the world sustins in ignoring them. So long as humanity blindly follows the precepts of the so-called world-teachers, who, according to their own followers, seem to have long groped for the Supreme basic principle of the universe, but who, finding at last their efforts fruitless, gave them up in despir, and took to preaching what they deemed best under such circumstances; so long the humanity can have no true conception of the Personality of God, nor can they know anything definite of Him to fix their mind upon. Without a form physical or ideal before him, man will never be able to think of God in the true sense of the term. It is this necessity of his nature that makes him create from his crude imagination a multitude of fantestic form and ascribe them to God. But God has a personality of his own which man can never perceive, unless God himself appears before him and reveals it by His grace. In the case of St.Manickavachakar and St. Thirujnana Sambandar we have strong internal evidence in their hymns to establish that God appeared before them in the form of a respledent person uniting in itself the rose-coloured side of the father and the blue coloured side of the mother. I have given a scientific treatment of this dual aspect of Godhead in my paper on ‘The conception of God as Rudra’ and so I do not like to enter agin into this subject in a preface like this. To the reader it will be now plin how clear/ how definite has been the manifestation of God’s Personality, it would be better for mankind to meditate on His gracious form that revealed itself to Manickavachakar and is described by him in vivid and unambiguous words. For it is plinly lid down in the Isavasyopanished that those who worship the forms and images of their own make mistaking them for the real Person of God, enter, at their death, into the regions of blinding darkness. Therefore let those who hanker after the salvation of their souls, accomplish it by a whole-hearted devotion of their mind to the contemplation of the one Almightly Parent of the universe as He is depicted in the Sacred Utterances of St.Manickavachakar.
Now, with the object of presenting the life of Manickavachakar in the form in Which it discloses itself incidentally in his own hymns addressed to Lord Siva, I Studied his ‘Thiruvachakam’ and Thiruchitrambalakkoviyar’ for days and nights together in a way quite differnt from that in which his Puranic biographers and others have viewed it. And the method of my study will be clearly seen in the extreme closeness in which the following narrative is bound up with the quotations taken from the two poems just mentioned. In spite of my ceaseless efforts to enter fully into a description of the life and experiences of our Saint, I cannot clim to have exhusted the subject in the large work that follows. As my study deepens, new facts are dily coming into light, and I cannot hope to embody them all in a single treatise of this kind. if God be willing I may be spared to publish  the present work in a much more enlarged form, samy, in three or four volumes.
when I first entered upon this work some seven or eight years ago, I never thought that the work would swell to its present size of more than thousand pages. Within the last twenty seven years from the time when my interest was first kindled in the investigation of our Saint’ Life and Times, many a magazine - article many a book and booklet, came from able hands, and most of these I had to study and criticize from time to time, in order to reach definite and positive conclusions as regards the two. This was how the work increased to its persent size. After so careful and comprehensive  a study of the subject, it gives me no small pleasure to find that my position remins the same as it was nearly three decades back, that nothing that came in the interval from any has shaken it in the least, and that other theories, however antagonistic to my own, have, when viewed in their proper perspective, only tended in the end to bring the truth of my conclusions into fuller relief. My study of the evidences then led me to fix the age of Manickavachakar in the third century A.D., and now the same conclusion is ratified by further evidences that have come to light during this long interval.
To the following Tamil work I have prefixed an English introduction epitomizing the salient points in the Life and Times of our Saint, so that it may serve as a guide to the English knowing students who do not possess a scholarly knowledge of Tamil but who ardently wish to acquaint themselves with the treasures in Tamil literature. It is my belief that those who have imbibed English education can appreciate better the method of historical criticism used in this book than most of the tamil pandits who do not and want not to know what is history, and what is myth.
Finally I wish to call the attention of the reader to the pure Tamil style of the treatise which is entirely free from Sanscrit and other foreign words. Let him judge from it whehter a living and at the same time a very ancient language, which can deal with all the higher aspects of human thought, without borrowing words from any other language, can be called poor and ordinary. Let him remember that Tamil is the only living language on his globe that unites in itself the glories of the vanished past with the culture of the living present. And let him therefore endeavour his best to spread Tamil learning and uplift his Tamil brethren in the social, moral, intellectural and religious culture that is within the easy reach of them all.
The sared Order of love,
pallavaram,
22nd January,1930.									    VEDACHALAM
